Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose I’m here because... well, honestly, it’s been a very long few months, and it feels like I’m the only one who actually sees what’s going on. I manage an entire office, you know? I oversee administrative staff all day long, so I know what a simple mistake looks like, and I know what a pattern looks like. And this—what’s happening with my sister—is a pattern. 

For months now, I’ve been keeping track. Her doctor's appointments get moved without anyone telling me. I’ll call to confirm, and suddenly the time is changed, or the day is changed, and the receptionist will just say, "Oh, it must have been a scheduling error." But it’s not just that. The bills for her care keep showing up wrong—always just slightly off, always in a way that requires me to spend hours on the phone trying to fix it. And then, when I do call, people conveniently "forget" to call me back. I leave messages, detailed messages, and nothing. Silence. 

Everyone around me—my family, the staff at the clinic—they all keep telling me, "Oh, it’s just clerical errors, it’s just the system being slow, people are busy." But I know better. I’ve been doing administrative work for decades. I have a degree in this kind of organizational management. I know when someone is deliberately trying to keep information from me. They are coordinating this. They want to push me out of the decisions regarding her care, and they’re doing it by making it impossible for me to keep track of anything. It’s exhausting, frankly, having to be on high alert all the time because no one else will admit what’s actually happening.
=====================================

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hearing or seeing things? Like... you mean like hallucinations? No. No, absolutely not. I want to be very, very clear about that, Doctor. My mind is completely sound. You don't... well, you don't manage an entire administrative department for over twenty years, overseeing fifteen different people and coordinating everything we do, if you're imagining things. 

I don't hear voices whispering to me, and I don't see shadows or visions or anything like that. What I see is... I see the actual, physical evidence. I see the timestamp on an email that they deliberately sent at 4:59 PM on a Friday so I wouldn't catch the appointment change until Monday morning. I see the printed invoices where the billing code was swapped—just one digit, mind you, but I know administrative systems, and I know that one digit is exactly enough to trigger an automatic rejection from the insurance company. That's what I'm seeing. I'm seeing cold, hard facts. 

People—especially my family, who frankly just don't want to deal with the hassle of it all—they try to make it sound like I'm just stressed and making things up in my head. But I have the paperwork. I actually started keeping a binder, color-coded by the way, with every single discrepancy, every "clerical error" from the clinic. So, no, I am definitely not seeing things that aren't there. I am seeing exactly what *is* there. I'm just the only one actually paying attention to the paper trail they're leaving behind.

I see the printed invoices where the billing code was swapped—just one digit, mind you, but I know administrative systems, and I know that one digit is exactly enough to trigger an automatic rejection from the insurance company. That's what I'm seeing. I'm seeing cold, hard facts. \n\nPeople—especially my family, who frankly just don't want to deal with the hassle of it all—they try to make it sound like I'm just stressed and making things up in my head. But I have the paperwork. I actually started keeping a binder, color-coded by the way, with every single discrepancy, every \"clerical error\" from the clinic. So, no, I am definitely not seeing things that aren't there. I am seeing exactly what *is* there. I'm just the only one actually paying attention to the paper trail they're leaving behind.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Well, if you really want to trace it back to the very beginning... I'd say it's been about 11 months now. Yes, exactly 11 months. It all started right around the time my sister's health took that sudden turn for the worse. Before that, she was managing fairly well on her own, but then... well, things declined rapidly, and someone had to step in and take charge. And since I'm the one with the organizational background, the one who actually knows how to navigate these labyrinthine healthcare systems, it naturally fell to me to start coordinating her care. 

At first, I thought the issues were just the usual bureaucratic incompetence. You know how it is, you expect a certain level of inefficiency when you're dealing with large medical networks. So for the first few weeks, maybe even the first month, I was giving them the benefit of the doubt. I was patient. I was polite. I made my little notes and made the phone calls to correct their "oversights." 

But then, as the weeks turned into months, the sheer volume of these "mistakes" became statistically impossible. I mean, I ran the numbers, Doctor. I literally sat down one evening with my calculator and the billing statements from the first three months, and the probability of that many consecutive errors happening by pure chance is astronomical. It was around that point—maybe two or three months into taking over her care—that I realized this wasn't incompetence. It was intentional. It was a coordinated effort to sideline me. So, yes, the interference has been happening for 11 months, practically from the moment I stepped in, but my absolute certainty that it's deliberate? That crystallized a few months into the process, once the pattern became undeniable. And it has only escalated since then. Every month, a new tactic. Every month, a new hurdle they throw in my path just to see if I'll trip. But I haven't. I've been tracking it all. For 11 months, I haven't missed a single thing.

I'm just the only one actually paying attention to the paper trail they're leaving behind.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been convinced that people are deliberately interfering with your sister’s care?\n\nPatient: Well, if you really want to trace it back to the very beginning... I'd say it's been about 11 months now. Yes, exactly 11 months. It all started right around the time my sister's health took that sudden turn for the worse. Before that, she was managing fairly well on her own, but then... well, things declined rapidly, and someone had to step in and take charge. And since I'm the one with the organizational background, the one who actually knows how to navigate these labyrinthine healthcare systems, it naturally fell to me to start coordinating her care. \n\nAt first, I thought the issues were just the usual bureaucratic incompetence. You know how it is, you expect a certain level of inefficiency when you're dealing with large medical networks. So for the first few weeks, maybe even the first month, I was giving them the benefit of the doubt. I was patient. I was polite. I made my little notes and made the phone calls to correct their \"oversights.\" \n\nBut then, as the weeks turned into months, the sheer volume of these \"mistakes\" became statistically impossible. I mean, I ran the numbers, Doctor. I literally sat down one evening with my calculator and the billing statements from the first three months, and the probability of that many consecutive errors happening by pure chance is astronomical. It was around that point—maybe two or three months into taking over her care—that I realized this wasn't incompetence. It was intentional. It was a coordinated effort to sideline me. So, yes, the interference has been happening for 11 months, practically from the moment I stepped in, but my absolute certainty that it's deliberate? That crystallized a few months into the process, once the pattern became undeniable. And it has only escalated since then. Every month, a new tactic. Every month, a new hurdle they throw in my path just to see if I'll trip. But I haven't. I've been tracking it all. For 11 months, I haven't missed a single thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Suddenly? Oh, no. No, no, no. It wasn’t like waking up one morning and having some sort of... epiphany. That’s just not how my mind works, Doctor. As I said, I’m an administrative professional. We don't jump to conclusions, we look at the data. So it wasn't a sudden flash of insight at all.

There was this one girl at the front desk—I think her name badge said Sarah, or maybe it was Tara, anyway, she always seemed terribly flustered—and I thought, 'She’s just overwhelmed, it’s a busy clinic, these things happen.' So I gave them grace. I really did. But then... well, it was a slow burn, really. A very gradual awakening to the reality of the situation. 

You see, when you manage people for as long as I have, you learn the difference between a one-off human error and a systemic, intentional issue. I would sit at my dining room table—I have this large oak table that I use as my workspace when I bring things home from the office—and I would just spread out all the bills, the appointment cards, my phone logs... absolutely everything. And I would just look at the timeline. It was like putting together a jigsaw puzzle where someone has intentionally hidden the corner pieces from you. You don't see the whole image all at once. You just slowly, methodically realize that the pieces you *do* have are forming a very specific, very deliberate picture. 

So, no, definitely not sudden. I think it’s very, very important that you understand that about me. I didn't just snap and decide they were interfering. I followed the paper trail, step by step, week by week, until the conclusion was simply unavoidable.

Silence. \n\nEveryone around me—my family, the staff at the clinic—they all keep telling me, \"Oh, it’s just clerical errors, it’s just the system being slow, people are busy.\" But I know better. I’ve been doing administrative work for decades. I have a degree in this kind of organizational management. I know when someone is deliberately trying to keep information from me. They are coordinating this. They want to push me out of the decisions regarding her care, and they’re doing it by making it impossible for me to keep track of anything. It’s exhausting, frankly, having to be on high alert all the time because no one else will admit what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don’t?\n\nPatient: Hearing or seeing things? Like... you mean like hallucinations? No. No, absolutely not. I want to be very, very clear about that, Doctor. My mind is completely sound. You don't... well, you don't manage an entire administrative department for over twenty years, overseeing fifteen different people and coordinating everything we do, if you're imagining things. \n\nI don't hear voices whispering to me, and I don't see shadows or visions or anything like that. What I see is... I see the actual, physical evidence. I see the timestamp on an email that they deliberately sent at 4:59 PM on a Friday so I wouldn't catch the appointment change until Monday morning. I see the printed invoices where the billing code was swapped—just one digit, mind you, but I know administrative systems, and I know that one digit is exactly enough to trigger an automatic rejection from the insurance company. That's what I'm seeing. I'm seeing cold, hard facts. \n\nPeople—especially my family, who frankly just don't want to deal with the hassle of it all—they try to make it sound like I'm just stressed and making things up in my head. But I have the paperwork. I actually started keeping a binder, color-coded by the way, with every single discrepancy, every \"clerical error\" from the clinic. So, no, I am definitely not seeing things that aren't there. I am seeing exactly what *is* there. I'm just the only one actually paying attention to the paper trail they're leaving behind.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been convinced that people are deliberately interfering with your sister’s care?\n\nPatient: Well, if you really want to trace it back to the very beginning... I'd say it's been about 11 months now. Yes, exactly 11 months. It all started right around the time my sister's health took that sudden turn for the worse. Before that, she was managing fairly well on her own, but then... well, things declined rapidly, and someone had to step in and take charge. And since I'm the one with the organizational background, the one who actually knows how to navigate these labyrinthine healthcare systems, it naturally fell to me to start coordinating her care. \n\nAt first, I thought the issues were just the usual bureaucratic incompetence. You know how it is, you expect a certain level of inefficiency when you're dealing with large medical networks. So for the first few weeks, maybe even the first month, I was giving them the benefit of the doubt. I was patient. I was polite. I made my little notes and made the phone calls to correct their \"oversights.\" \n\nBut then, as the weeks turned into months, the sheer volume of these \"mistakes\" became statistically impossible. I mean, I ran the numbers, Doctor. I literally sat down one evening with my calculator and the billing statements from the first three months, and the probability of that many consecutive errors happening by pure chance is astronomical. It was around that point—maybe two or three months into taking over her care—that I realized this wasn't incompetence. It was intentional. It was a coordinated effort to sideline me. So, yes, the interference has been happening for 11 months, practically from the moment I stepped in, but my absolute certainty that it's deliberate? That crystallized a few months into the process, once the pattern became undeniable. And it has only escalated since then. Every month, a new tactic. Every month, a new hurdle they throw in my path just to see if I'll trip. But I haven't. I've been tracking it all. For 11 months, I haven't missed a single thing.\n\nDoctor: Did this belief that people are deliberately interfering with your sister’s care come on very suddenly?\n\nPatient: Suddenly? Oh, no. No, no, no. It wasn’t like waking up one morning and having some sort of... epiphany. That’s just not how my mind works, Doctor. As I said, I’m an administrative professional. We don't jump to conclusions, we look at the data. So it wasn't a sudden flash of insight at all. \n\nLike I was explaining before, for those first few weeks, I was actually making excuses for them! I remember thinking, 'Well, maybe the receptionist is new.' There was this one girl at the front desk—I think her name badge said Sarah, or maybe it was Tara, anyway, she always seemed terribly flustered—and I thought, 'She’s just overwhelmed, it’s a busy clinic, these things happen.' So I gave them grace. I really did. But then... well, it was a slow burn, really. A very gradual awakening to the reality of the situation. \n\nYou see, when you manage people for as long as I have, you learn the difference between a one-off human error and a systemic, intentional issue. I would sit at my dining room table—I have this large oak table that I use as my workspace when I bring things home from the office—and I would just spread out all the bills, the appointment cards, my phone logs... absolutely everything. And I would just look at the timeline. It was like putting together a jigsaw puzzle where someone has intentionally hidden the corner pieces from you. You don't see the whole image all at once. You just slowly, methodically realize that the pieces you *do* have are forming a very specific, very deliberate picture. \n\nSo, no, definitely not sudden. I think it’s very, very important that you understand that about me. I didn't just snap and decide they were interfering. I followed the paper trail, step by step, week by week, until the conclusion was simply unavoidable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Distrust other people's motives? Oh, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all. In fact, if you asked anyone at the office—well, anyone who’s worked under me for more than a year—they’d tell you I’m incredibly fair. You have to be, in administration. You can't run a department of fifteen people if you're constantly second-guessing every time someone asks for a sick day or requests a budget extension. 

I’ve always been someone who operates on the assumption that people are generally trying to do their jobs correctly. I mean, obviously, I verify things. Trust, but verify, as they say. If someone submits an expense report, I check the receipts. But that’s not distrust, Doctor, that’s just basic competence. That’s just doing my job. I never used to look at a misplaced file or a scheduling conflict and think, 'Aha, they’re out to get me!' I would just think, 'Well, Susan in accounting missed her morning coffee, let's just fix this and move on.' 

Before this whole nightmare with the clinic started, I was perfectly content to believe that the systems we have in place—whether it’s corporate administration or healthcare—were functioning as intended. I never had this... this feeling of being targeted. I never had to keep a color-coded binder just to prove I wasn't losing my mind! I trusted the process. I trusted that if I filled out the forms correctly, the outcome would be correct. 

So, no, I wasn't walking around suspicious of everyone. That's what makes this whole situation so incredibly frustrating. I didn't want this. I didn't go looking for a conspiracy, or whatever you want to call it. I was perfectly happy just managing my department, taking care of my own life, trusting that other professionals would do the same. It’s only because the evidence with my sister’s care became so overwhelming, so blatantly obvious, that I had to change my perspective. I didn't change, Doctor. The circumstances changed, and I just had to adapt to the reality of what they were doing.

Every month, a new hurdle they throw in my path just to see if I'll trip. But I haven't. I've been tracking it all. For 11 months, I haven't missed a single thing.\n\nDoctor: Did this belief that people are deliberately interfering with your sister’s care come on very suddenly?\n\nPatient: Suddenly? Oh, no. No, no, no. It wasn’t like waking up one morning and having some sort of... epiphany. That’s just not how my mind works, Doctor. As I said, I’m an administrative professional. We don't jump to conclusions, we look at the data. So it wasn't a sudden flash of insight at all. \n\nLike I was explaining before, for those first few weeks, I was actually making excuses for them! I remember thinking, 'Well, maybe the receptionist is new.' There was this one girl at the front desk—I think her name badge said Sarah, or maybe it was Tara, anyway, she always seemed terribly flustered—and I thought, 'She’s just overwhelmed, it’s a busy clinic, these things happen.' So I gave them grace. I really did. But then... well, it was a slow burn, really. A very gradual awakening to the reality of the situation. \n\nYou see, when you manage people for as long as I have, you learn the difference between a one-off human error and a systemic, intentional issue. I would sit at my dining room table—I have this large oak table that I use as my workspace when I bring things home from the office—and I would just spread out all the bills, the appointment cards, my phone logs... absolutely everything. And I would just look at the timeline. It was like putting together a jigsaw puzzle where someone has intentionally hidden the corner pieces from you. You don't see the whole image all at once. You just slowly, methodically realize that the pieces you *do* have are forming a very specific, very deliberate picture. \n\nSo, no, definitely not sudden. I think it’s very, very important that you understand that about me. I didn't just snap and decide they were interfering. I followed the paper trail, step by step, week by week, until the conclusion was simply unavoidable.\n\nDoctor: Before all of this with your sister began, did you generally tend to distrust other people’s motives?\n\nPatient: Distrust other people's motives? Oh, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all. In fact, if you asked anyone at the office—well, anyone who’s worked under me for more than a year—they’d tell you I’m incredibly fair. You have to be, in administration. You can't run a department of fifteen people if you're constantly second-guessing every time someone asks for a sick day or requests a budget extension. \n\nI’ve always been someone who operates on the assumption that people are generally trying to do their jobs correctly. I mean, obviously, I verify things. Trust, but verify, as they say. If someone submits an expense report, I check the receipts. But that’s not distrust, Doctor, that’s just basic competence. That’s just doing my job. I never used to look at a misplaced file or a scheduling conflict and think, 'Aha, they’re out to get me!' I would just think, 'Well, Susan in accounting missed her morning coffee, let's just fix this and move on.' \n\nBefore this whole nightmare with the clinic started, I was perfectly content to believe that the systems we have in place—whether it’s corporate administration or healthcare—were functioning as intended. I never had this... this feeling of being targeted. I never had to keep a color-coded binder just to prove I wasn't losing my mind! I trusted the process. I trusted that if I filled out the forms correctly, the outcome would be correct. \n\nSo, no, I wasn't walking around suspicious of everyone. That's what makes this whole situation so incredibly frustrating. I didn't want this. I didn't go looking for a conspiracy, or whatever you want to call it. I was perfectly happy just managing my department, taking care of my own life, trusting that other professionals would do the same. It’s only because the evidence with my sister’s care became so overwhelming, so blatantly obvious, that I had to change my perspective. I didn't change, Doctor. The circumstances changed, and I just had to adapt to the reality of what they were doing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

The circumstances changed, and I just had to adapt to the reality of what they were doing.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had the feeling that your thoughts were being controlled by someone else?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / first_line_supervisor_of_office_or_administrative_support_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Controlled by someone else? My thoughts? No, no, absolutely not. I mean, really, Doctor, if someone else were controlling my thoughts, do you think I'd be spending my evenings meticulously cross-referencing insurance codes and highlighting billing discrepancies? No, I am entirely in control of my own faculties. That's precisely why this whole situation is so infuriating to me. I am a rational, organized person. I manage an entire department of administrative staff, for goodness sake! You don't get to my level by letting other people dictate what goes on in your own head.

My thoughts are my own. They always have been. When I sit down at my oak table and look at the paperwork, the conclusions I draw are based on the data in front of me, not some sort of... external influence. I'm the one connecting the dots. I'm the one seeing the patterns. If anything, the people at the clinic are trying to *confuse* my thoughts, trying to gaslight me with their 'lost' files and 'accidental' scheduling errors, but they aren't controlling them. They wish they could! It would make their little campaign of interference so much easier if they could just flip a switch in my brain and make me docile and compliant. But they can't. I'm too sharp for that. I see exactly what they're doing, and it's my own mind, my own sharp, administrative mind, that figured it out. So, no, definitely no mind control here. Just good, old-fashioned critical thinking.

And this—what’s happening with my sister—is a pattern. \n\nFor months now, I’ve been keeping track. Her doctor's appointments get moved without anyone telling me. I’ll call to confirm, and suddenly the time is changed, or the day is changed, and the receptionist will just say, \"Oh, it must have been a scheduling error.\" But it’s not just that. The bills for her care keep showing up wrong—always just slightly off, always in a way that requires me to spend hours on the phone trying to fix it. They are coordinating this. They want to push me out of the decisions regarding her care, and they’re doing it by making it impossible for me to keep track of anything. I see the printed invoices where the billing code was swapped—just one digit, mind you, but I know administrative systems, and I know that one digit is exactly enough to trigger an automatic rejection from the insurance company. That's what I'm seeing. I'm seeing cold, hard facts. \n\nPeople—especially my family, who frankly just don't want to deal with the hassle of it all—they try to make it sound like I'm just stressed and making things up in my head. So, no, I am definitely not seeing things that aren't there. I am seeing exactly what *is* there. I'm just the only one actually paying attention to the paper trail they're leaving behind.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been convinced that people are deliberately interfering with your sister’s care?\n\nPatient: Well, if you really want to trace it back to the very beginning... I'd say it's been about 11 months now. Yes, exactly 11 months. It all started right around the time my sister's health took that sudden turn for the worse. Before that, she was managing fairly well on her own, but then... well, things declined rapidly, and someone had to step in and take charge. And since I'm the one with the organizational background, the one who actually knows how to navigate these labyrinthine healthcare systems, it naturally fell to me to start coordinating her care. \n\nAt first, I thought the issues were just the usual bureaucratic incompetence. You know how it is, you expect a certain level of inefficiency when you're dealing with large medical networks. So for the first few weeks, maybe even the first month, I was giving them the benefit of the doubt. I was patient. I was polite. I made my little notes and made the phone calls to correct their \"oversights.\" \n\nBut then, as the weeks turned into months, the sheer volume of these \"mistakes\" became statistically impossible. I mean, I ran the numbers, Doctor. I literally sat down one evening with my calculator and the billing statements from the first three months, and the probability of that many consecutive errors happening by pure chance is astronomical. It was around that point—maybe two or three months into taking over her care—that I realized this wasn't incompetence. It was intentional. It was a coordinated effort to sideline me. So, yes, the interference has been happening for 11 months, practically from the moment I stepped in, but my absolute certainty that it's deliberate? That crystallized a few months into the process, once the pattern became undeniable. And it has only escalated since then. Every month, a new tactic. Every month, a new hurdle they throw in my path just to see if I'll trip. But I haven't. I've been tracking it all. For 11 months, I haven't missed a single thing.\n\nDoctor: Did this belief that people are deliberately interfering with your sister’s care come on very suddenly?\n\nPatient: Suddenly? Oh, no. No, no, no. It wasn’t like waking up one morning and having some sort of... epiphany. That’s just not how my mind works, Doctor. As I said, I’m an administrative professional. We don't jump to conclusions, we look at the data. So it wasn't a sudden flash of insight at all. \n\nLike I was explaining before, for those first few weeks, I was actually making excuses for them! I remember thinking, 'Well, maybe the receptionist is new.' There was this one girl at the front desk—I think her name badge said Sarah, or maybe it was Tara, anyway, she always seemed terribly flustered—and I thought, 'She’s just overwhelmed, it’s a busy clinic, these things happen.' So I gave them grace. I really did. But then... well, it was a slow burn, really. A very gradual awakening to the reality of the situation. \n\nYou see, when you manage people for as long as I have, you learn the difference between a one-off human error and a systemic, intentional issue. I would sit at my dining room table—I have this large oak table that I use as my workspace when I bring things home from the office—and I would just spread out all the bills, the appointment cards, my phone logs... absolutely everything. And I would just look at the timeline. It was like putting together a jigsaw puzzle where someone has intentionally hidden the corner pieces from you. You don't see the whole image all at once. You just slowly, methodically realize that the pieces you *do* have are forming a very specific, very deliberate picture. \n\nSo, no, definitely not sudden. I think it’s very, very important that you understand that about me. I didn't just snap and decide they were interfering. Oh, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all. In fact, if you asked anyone at the office—well, anyone who’s worked under me for more than a year—they’d tell you I’m incredibly fair. That’s just doing my job. I never used to look at a misplaced file or a scheduling conflict and think, 'Aha, they’re out to get me!' I would just think, 'Well, Susan in accounting missed her morning coffee, let's just fix this and move on.' \n\nBefore this whole nightmare with the clinic started, I was perfectly content to believe that the systems we have in place—whether it’s corporate administration or healthcare—were functioning as intended. I never had this... this feeling of being targeted. I never had to keep a color-coded binder just to prove I wasn't losing my mind! I trusted the process. I trusted that if I filled out the forms correctly, the outcome would be correct. \n\nSo, no, I wasn't walking around suspicious of everyone. That's what makes this whole situation so incredibly frustrating. I didn't want this. I didn't go looking for a conspiracy, or whatever you want to call it. [
  {
    "role": "system",

Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, I suppose I’m here because... well, honestly, it’s been a very long few months, and it feels like I’m the only one who actually sees what’s going on. I manage an entire office, you know? I oversee administrative staff all day long, so I know what a simple mistake looks like, and I know what a pattern looks like. And this—what’s happening with my sister—is a pattern. \n\nFor months now, I’ve been keeping track. Her doctor's appointments get moved without anyone telling me. I’ll call to confirm, and suddenly the time is changed, or the day is changed, and the receptionist will just say, \"Oh, it must have been a scheduling error.\" But it’s not just that. The bills for her care keep showing up wrong—always just slightly off, always in a way that requires me to spend hours on the phone trying to fix it. And then, when I do call, people conveniently \"forget\" to call me back. I leave messages, detailed messages, and nothing. Silence. \n\nEveryone around me—my family, the staff at the clinic—they all keep telling me, \"Oh, it’s just clerical errors, it’s just the system being slow, people are busy.\" But I know better. I’ve been doing administrative work for decades. I have a degree in this kind of organizational management. I know when someone is deliberately trying to keep information from me. They are coordinating this. They want to push me out of the decisions regarding her care, and they’re doing it by making it impossible for me to keep track of anything. It’s exhausting, frankly, having to be on high alert all the time because no one else will admit what’s actually happening.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nHearing or seeing things? Like... you mean like hallucinations? No. No, absolutely not. I want to be very, very clear about that, Doctor. My mind is completely sound. You don't... well, you don't manage an entire administrative department for over twenty years, overseeing fifteen different people and coordinating everything we do, if you're imagining things. \n\nI don't hear voices whispering to me, and I don't see shadows or visions or anything like that. What I see is... I see the actual, physical evidence. I see the timestamp on an email that they deliberately sent at 4:59 PM on a Friday so I wouldn't catch the appointment change until Monday morning. I see the printed invoices where the billing code was swapped—just one digit, mind you, but I know administrative systems, and I know that one digit is exactly enough to trigger an automatic rejection from the insurance company. That's what I'm seeing. I'm seeing cold, hard facts. \n\nPeople—especially my family, who frankly just don't want to deal with the hassle of it all—they try to make it sound like I'm just stressed and making things up in my head. But I have the paperwork. I actually started keeping a binder, color-coded by the way, with every single discrepancy, every \"clerical error\" from the clinic. So, no, I am definitely not seeing things that aren't there. I am seeing exactly what *is* there. I'm just the only one actually paying attention to the paper trail they're leaving behind.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, if you really want to trace it back to the very beginning... I'd say it's been about 11 months now. Yes, exactly 11 months. It all started right around the time my sister's health took that sudden turn for the worse. Before that, she was managing fairly well on her own, but then... well, things declined rapidly, and someone had to step in and take charge. And since I'm the one with the organizational background, the one who actually knows how to navigate these labyrinthine healthcare systems, it naturally fell to me to start coordinating her care. \n\nAt first, I thought the issues were just the usual bureaucratic incompetence. You know how it is, you expect a certain level of inefficiency when you're dealing with large medical networks. So for the first few weeks, maybe even the first month, I was giving them the benefit of the doubt. I was patient. I was polite. I made my little notes and made the phone calls to correct their \"oversights.\" \n\nBut then, as the weeks turned into months, the sheer volume of these \"mistakes\" became statistically impossible. I mean, I ran the numbers, Doctor. I literally sat down one evening with my calculator and the billing statements from the first three months, and the probability of that many consecutive errors happening by pure chance is astronomical. It was around that point—maybe two or three months into taking over her care—that I realized this wasn't incompetence. It was intentional. It was a coordinated effort to sideline me. So, yes, the interference has been happening for 11 months, practically from the moment I stepped in, but my absolute certainty that it's deliberate? That crystallized a few months into the process, once the pattern became undeniable. And it has only escalated since then. Every month, a new tactic. Every month, a new hurdle they throw in my path just to see if I'll trip. But I haven't. I've been tracking it all. For 11 months, I haven't missed a single thing.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. And I would just look at the timeline. It was like putting together a jigsaw puzzle where someone has intentionally hidden the corner pieces from you. You don't see the whole image all at once. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.
